Q. 
What principles guided the development of your 
1 
recommended budget? 
2 
A. 
Affordability and project justification.  
3 
Generally, I am concerned with the trajectory of 
4 
IT spending proposed by Con Edison and how that 
5 
would impact customers’ ability to afford 
6 
delivery rates.  In addition, the Company has 
7 
the responsibility of justifying its proposal, 
8 
therefore for proposals that were found to be 
9 
justified, I recommend a level of funding; for 
10 
projects not justified, I recommend funding 
11 
reduction.  
12 
Q. 
How did you determine your recommended 
13 
adjustments? 
14 
A. 
I used three common adjustment methods.  The 
15 
most frequently applied adjustment is a 
16 
reduction of the capital and/or O&M budget to 70 
17 
percent of the proposed amount.  The second most 
18 
frequently applied adjustment reduces the 
19 
project budget to the average of historical 
20 
spending, inflated to 2026 dollars.  The third 
21 
most common adjustment reduces the project 
22 
budget to the amount indicated from a linear 
23 
forecast of available spending data.  All other 
24 


--- Page 9 ---
Cases 25-E-0072 and 25-G-0073 
 
ANDREW CARPENTER 
 
 
8  
 
adjustments are reductions to percents of the 
1 
capital budget proposed by the Company that 
2 
reflect other considerations, as provided.   
3 
Q. 
Please elaborate on what inflation factors were 
4 
used throughout your testimony when making your 
5 
calculations.   
6 
A. 
To inflate from 2020 to 2025 dollars, historical 
7 
values were multiplied by 1.2220.  To inflate 
8 
from 2021 to 2025 dollars, historical values 
9 
were multiplied by 1.1689.  To inflate from 2022 
10 
to 2025 dollars, historical values were 
11 
multiplied by 1.0909.  To inflate from 2023 to 
12 
2025 dollars, historical values were multiplied 
13 
by 1.0538.  To inflate from 2024 to 2025 
14 
dollars, historical values were multiplied by 
15 
1.0283.  To inflate from 2025 to 2026 dollars, 
16 
historical values were multiplied by 1.0266.  To 
17 
inflate from 2026 to 2027 dollars, historical 
18 
values were multiplied by 1.0240.  To inflate 
19 
from 2027 to 2028 dollars, historical values 
20 
were multiplied by 1.0220.  I used the inflation 
21 
factors from the forecasts of the Gross Domestic 
22 
Product – Price Index in the April 2024 Issue of 
23 
the Blue Chip Economic Indicators.  First, 
24 


--- Page 10 ---
Cases 25-E-0072 and 25-G-0073 
 
ANDREW CARPENTER 
 
 
9  
 
please elaborate on the rationale for adjusting 
1 
projects to 70 percent of the proposed value. 
2 
A. 
Adjusting projects to 70 percent of the proposed 
3 
value is a reasonable adjustment methodology for 
4 
five reasons.  First, the projects proposed by 
5 
the Company have, in the aggregate, considerably 
6 
unrefined scopes.  When projects have unrefined 
7 
defined scopes and have not fully determined the 
8 
business requirements driving project costs 
9 
adjustments must necessarily be arbitrary, 
10 
particularly when detailed estimations of 
11 
project costs have not yet been finalized.   
12 
Q. 
What is the second reason? 
13 
A. 
The second reason is that the budgets of 
14 
individual projects reflect, in part, 
15 
assumptions and considerations of other 
16 
projects; a precise adjustment to one project 
17 
can potentially decrease the precision of an 
18 
adjustment to another project. 
19 
Q. 
What is the third reason? 
20 
A. 
The third reason is that projects with 
21 
relatively more refined scopes do not 
22 
necessarily break down costs in a manner that 
23 
enables a precise adjustment.  For example, in 
24 


--- Page 11 ---
Cases 25-E-0072 and 25-G-0073 
 
ANDREW CARPENTER 
 
 
10  
 
response to DPS-933, question 2b , which 
1 
requests the capital, and operational and 
2 
maintenance costs for the activities identified 
3 
in the White Paper "Customer Care & Billing 
4 
Enhancements and Upgrade", an on-going project, 
5 
the Company stated: "The Company does not track 
6 
costs at the activity level."  Similarly, in 
7 
response to DPS-984, which requested the capital 
8 
and operational expenses associated with each 
9 
focus area in the planned "Customer Data 
10 
Sharing" project, the Company stated: "As the 
11 
Customer Data Sharing program leverages a 
12 
customer-centric, agile project management 
13 
approach, project scopes are continually 
14 
adapting based on customer feedback, analytics, 
15 
and regulatory priorities. For this reason, and 
16 
to maximize flexibility and efficiency, Customer 
17 
Data Sharing breaks down its costs by element of 
18 
expense – including labor, contract services, 
19 
and overhead – rather than by project component 
20 
or focus area."   
21 
Q. 
What is the fourth reason? 
22 
A. 
The fourth reason is that, as I will discuss, 
23 
the projects categorized as "Operationally 
24 


--- Page 12 ---
Cases 25-E-0072 and 25-G-0073 
 
ANDREW CARPENTER 
 
 
11  
 
Required" contain project elements best 
1 
understood at strategic elements, and the 
2 
Company is not always capable of providing 
3 
project costs parsed by work category.  For 
4 
example, in response to DPS-951, which requested 
5 
the program costs parsed by work category for 
6 
the "AMI Systems Sustainability Program"--which 
7 
is categorized by the Company as operationally 
8 
required, strategic, and regulatory mandated--
9 
the Company states “AMI Systems Sustainability 
10 
Program consists of multiple workstreams 
11 
including not only the billing functions, but 
12 
also safety of customers and services for 
13 
operations, that are designed to meet regulatory 
14 
mandates, operational needs, and strategic 
15 
goals. It is difficult to separate work and cost 
16 
estimates based on these categories because the 
17 
work done covers all categories.”  However, in 
18 
response to DPS-970, the Company provided the 
19 
costs associated with the IT System COE project 
20 
parsed by work category, and the strategic costs 
21 
accounted for approximately 30 percent of the 
22 
total costs--which means a reduction to 70 
23 
percent accurately reflects the removal of 
24 


--- Page 13 ---
Cases 25-E-0072 and 25-G-0073 
 
ANDREW CARPENTER 
 
 
12  
 
strategic elements.   
1 
Q. 
What is the fifth reason? 
2 
A. 
The fifth reason is that some project budgets 
3 
estimations are primarily based on the costs 
4 
incurred for projects the Company considers 
5 
sufficiently similar.  For example, the 
6 
"Customer Recommendation & Analysis Tools" 
7 
project provides the budget estimation 
8

16 
because both projects support the digital

18

19 
potential costs, such as for customer research, 
20 
utilizing full-time or contract staff in roles

such as product owners, business analysts,

front-end and back-end developers, UI/UX, copy, 
23 
system integrations, and testing."  Overall, the 
24 


--- Page 14 ---
Cases 25-E-0072 and 25-G-0073 
 
ANDREW CARPENTER 
 
 
13  
 
methodology and information provided does not 
1 
enable precise adjustments to project budgets 
2 
and such circumstances make a reduction to 70 
3 
percent a reasonable uniform budget adjustment 
4 
method. 
5 
Q. 
Elaborate next on the reduction of project 
6 
budget to the average of historical spending, 
7 
inflated to 2026 dollars. 
8 
A. 
For all projects and programs proposed by the 
9 
Company’s IT Panel, where historical spend 
10 
information was provided, I calculated and 
11 
analyzed the average capital and O&M costs, 
12 
adjusted for inflation, and inflated that amount 
13 
to 2026 dollars.  This amount, absent other 
14 
circumstances, should enable a project or 
15 
program to continue existing work processes but 
16 
reduce the development of strategic project 
17 
elements.  When applying this methodology to 
18 
establish my recommended funding level for 
19 
certain projects, the recommended budget for 
20 
Rate Year two is the Rate Year 1 recommended 
21 
budget inflated to 2027 dollars, and the 
22 
recommended budget for Rate Year three is the 
23 
Rate Year 2 recommended budget inflated to 2028 
24 


--- Page 15 ---
Cases 25-E-0072 and 25-G-0073 
 
ANDREW CARPENTER 
 
 
14  
 
dollars. 
1 
Q. 
Elaborate on the reduction of a project budget 
2 
to the amount indicated by a linear forecast. 
3 
A. 
A linear forecast draws a straight line with 
4 
available data and is a suitable method for 
5 
extrapolating spending trends.  This trend 
6 
incorporates actual real-world market conditions 
7 
and the Company's current practices and spending 
8 
patterns.  The amount provided by a linear 
9 
forecast analysis, absent other circumstances, 
10 
would enable a project or program to continue 
11 
existing work processes and incorporates 
12 
historical project growth.  When applying this 
13 
methodology to Rate Year two and rate year 
14 
three, the line drawn by the linear forecast 
15 
extends an additional two years. 
16 
Q. 
Where are these calculations presented? 
17 
A. 
These calculations are presented for each 
18 
project within Exhibit___(AC-3).

11 
Q. 
Generally, is the magnitude of the budget 
12 
requested by the IT Panel adequately justified? 
13 
A. 
No.  The IT Panel’s budget request endangers 
14 
current and future service affordability.   
15 
Q. 
How does the IT budget endanger current 
16 
affordability? 
17 
A. 
IT expenditures have a greater dollar-for-dollar 
18 
impact on the revenue requirement due to their, 
19 
on average, shorter service lives relative to 
20 
"traditional" utility assets such as 
21 
distribution poles or service lines.  Therefore, 
22 
IT expenditures are, on average, recovered from 
23 
customers over a shorter period than 
24 


--- Page 19 ---
Cases 25-E-0072 and 25-G-0073 
 
ANDREW CARPENTER 
 
 
18  
 
“traditional” utility assets.   
1 
Q. 
How does the IT Panel’s proposed budget endanger 
2 
future service affordability? 
3 
A. 
The Company states that the cost of operating 
4 
data centers is continuing to rise and 
5 
identifies various reasons why the cost of 
6 
operating IT is increasing in the IT Panel’s 
7 
exhibits and initial testimony, as previously 
8 
discussed.  One driver of this increasing 
9 
costliness is the need to integrate new systems 
10 
with existing systems.  The Company's IT 
11 
strategy is to consider this integration as an 
12 
on-going and continuous process, and thus it is 
13 
included as an element of multiple IT projects 
14 
and as the main component of the Integration 
15 
Technical Obsolescence project.  These 
16 
integration costs would increase if IT expands 
17 
its scope, applications, connections, and other 
18 
processes.   
19 
Q. 
Does the IT Panel’s proposed budget endanger 
20 
future service affordability in any other ways? 
21 
A. 
Yes.  On page 17 of the initial testimony of the 
22 
IT Panel, the Company states that incremental 
23 
O&M funding is needed “for the increasing 
24 


--- Page 20 ---
Cases 25-E-0072 and 25-G-0073 
 
ANDREW CARPENTER 
 
 
19  
 
maintenance and subscription contracts for 
1 
overall IT infrastructure” and indicates that 
2 
the modernization and improvement of data 
3 
centers is, in part, driving the need for 
4 
incremental O&M.  The Company also states that 
5 
“the costs associated with maintaining state-of-
6 
the-art data centers are on an upward 
7

Q. 
Can you define a "project element"? 
9 
A. 
A project element defines what is to be done 
10 
through the components of the project work or 
11 
project deliverable contained within the overall 
12 
project scope.   
13 
Q. 
Can you define an "insufficiently justified 
14 
project element" in the context of IT? 
15 
A. 
In the context of IT, a project element is 
16 
insufficiently justified when it meets two 
17 
criteria.  First, the project element is 
18 
unnecessary to maintain current operational 
19 
standards or to meet an operational standard 
20 
that is certain to arise as a necessity.  
21 
Second, the beneficial deliverable is not 
22 
substantially certain to outweigh the cost of 
23 
the project element.   
24 


--- Page 23 ---
Cases 25-E-0072 and 25-G-0073 
 
ANDREW CARPENTER 
 
 
22  
 
Q. 
What is necessary to establish that a beneficial 
1 
deliverable is substantially certain to outweigh 
2 
the cost of a project element?   
3 
A. 
For a beneficial deliverable to be substantially 
4 
certain to outweigh the cost, there must be 
5 
adequate evidence supporting the estimated 
6 
beneficial value of the deliverable, and 
7 
adequate evidence supporting the estimated costs 
8 
associated with the deliverable or overall 
9 
project.  Conversely, project elements with 
10 
inadequate evidence supporting the estimated 
11 
benefits, or inadequate evidence supporting the 
12 
estimated costs, cannot be reasonably understood 
13 
as sufficiently justified. 
14 
Q. 
Does the Company’s IT Panel propose unnecessary 
15 
or insufficiently justified project elements? 
16 
A. 
Yes.  All my recommended adjustments are rooted 
17 
in the elimination of insufficiently justified 
18 
project elements. 
19 
Q. 
What is necessary to establish that a beneficial 
20 
deliverable is substantially certain to outweigh 
21 
the cost of a project element?   
22 
A. 
For a beneficial deliverable to be substantially 
23 
certain to outweigh the cost, there must be

1 
Q. 
What is a breakage? 
2 
A. 
Breakage is a discrepancy among the exhibits, 
3 
testimony, and the supporting financial models.   
4 
Q. 
What is a consistency review? 
5 
A. 
A consistency review is the process of examining 
6 
information and cross referencing the 
7 
information with what is contained in other 
8 
sources.  This review is necessary to prove that 
9 
the information presented within exhibits and 
10 
testimony is congruent with the information 
11 
contained within the workpapers.   
12 
Q. 
What types of breakages did you encounter during 
13 
the consistency review? 
14 
A. 
I encountered four types of breakages, breakages 
15 
in values, breakages in identification, 
16 
breakages in classification, and breakages in 
17 
content. 
18 
Q. 
What is a breakage in value? 
19 
A. 
A breakage in value occurs when two documents 
20 
contain incongruent or otherwise unequal values. 
21

What breakages in value were identified? 
22 
A. 
In the exhibits originally filed by the Company,

there were breakages between the summary

testimony and values presented in Exhibit__(IT-

1) and Exhibit__(IT-2).

Q.

1

2

3

5

6

7

8

10

15

16

17

18

that would arise for each project.  As 
24

57  
 
previously discussed, the Company’s IT Panel’s

going reporting regarding Con Edison’s use of 
1

2

4 
Q.

11 
Q. 
Why do you recommend such reporting? 
12 
A. 
There are two immediate concerns with AI.  
13 
First, there is insufficient evidence to 
14 
conclude that Artificial Intelligence 
15 
investments are cost-effective.  Second, 
16 
Artificial Intelligence models require 
17 
substantial effort to validate.  AI technology 
18 
represents "black boxes" that require 
19 
significant effort by the technology user to 
20 
validate outputs.   
21 
Q. 
Elaborate on the lack of evidence supporting the 
22 
cost-effectiveness of Artificial Intelligence 
23 
investments. 
24 


--- Page 267 ---
Cases 25-E-0072 and 25-G-0073 
 
ANDREW CARPENTER 
 
 
266  
 
A. 
Artificial Intelligence is not free and requires 
1 
both significant computation during model 
2 
training and data storage to retain the model.  
3 
Obtaining, developing, and curating suitable 
4 
training data is another expense.  The Company 
5 
indicates in response to DPS-617 that model 
6 
training will be done "as needed to ensure 
7 
accuracy", which the Company indicates 
8 
"typically happens daily or weekly."  
9 
Additionally, the FTEs that develop and maintain 
10 
AI can be reasonably expected to be compensated 
11 
at a rate above the average utility employee.  
12 
Artificial Intelligence that is developed and 
13 
supplied by third-parties represents the newest 
14 
potential vector of escalating IT costs; if the 
15 
services providers increase cost, the Company 
16 
will be "locked in" by reliance on the model 
17 
provider.  There is no conclusive evidence in 
18 
the record that that benefits derived from 
19 
Artificial Intelligence will exceed the costs.  
20 
The avoided cost descriptions provided by the 
21 
Company for these projects in response to DPS-
22 
596 indicate that, in general, it is difficult 
23 
to quantify the savings and cost avoidance for 
24 


--- Page 268 ---
Cases 25-E-0072 and 25-G-0073 
 
ANDREW CARPENTER 
 
 
267  
 
these projects.  Where the Company does provide 
1 
a value, it is simultaneously imprecise and 
2 
insufficient to justify the project cost.   
3 
Q. 
Elaborate on the "black box" nature of AI and 
4 
the resources necessary to validate AI model 
5 
outputs. 
6 
A. 
The complexity and structure of AI models 
7 
renders establishing a precise understanding of 
8 
the underlying decision-making processes a 
9 
practical impossibility.  The Company speaks to 
10 
this on page 36 of Exhibit___(IT-9), within the 
11 
Data & AI Governance Program White Paper.  The 
12 
Company states that "AI introduces new ethical, 
13 
legal, and governance challenges, including 
14 
discrimination, biases, incorrect outcomes, and 
15 
lack of transparency".  This "lack of 
16 
transparency" is in part due to the "black box" 
17 
nature--inputs go into the black box, and 
18 
outputs come out.  In all contexts this "lack of 
19 
transparency" necessitates careful evaluation 
20 
and verification of inputs and outputs; a 
21 
process that parties are unlikely to be 
22 
permitted or capable of reasonably conducting.  
23 
As AI technologies are increasingly adopted, 
24 


--- Page 269 ---
Cases 25-E-0072 and 25-G-0073 
 
ANDREW CARPENTER 
 
 
268  
 
Company products, processes, and decisions will 
1 
be increasingly formed or otherwise influenced 
2 
by these "black boxes". 
3 
Q. 
Elaborate on the term “black box” and how the 
4 
term is applicable to AI systems. 
5 
A. 
Given that multiple definitions of “black box” 
6 
exist, for the purposes of this testimony, I 
7 
define three sub-types of “black boxes”: "true 
8 
black boxes", "locked boxes", and "opaque 
9 
boxes", which each represent information systems 
10 
with different characteristics. 
11 
Q. 
How do you define and use the term "black box"? 
12 
A. 
A "black box" is a simplified representation of 
13 
a process or system of processes; this 
14 
representation has three components: the 
15 
"inputs", which is the data or information 
16 
provided, the "black box" which executes some 
17 
unknown process or processes; and the "outputs", 
18 
data or information produced by the "black box" 
19 
from the “inputs”.   
20 
Q. 
Elaborate and define the term "locked box". 
21 
A. 
As the term "black box" is applicable to systems 
22 
analysis, the term can be used colloquially to 
23 
refer to a parties’ immediate ascertainable 
24 


--- Page 270 ---
Cases 25-E-0072 and 25-G-0073 
 
ANDREW CARPENTER 
 
 
269  
 
perspective given presently available data and 
1 
information.  This means that, for example, if 
2 
the Company files certain information 
3 
confidentially that is critical to the 
4 
understanding of some system or process, parties 
5 
with access to confidential information are 
6 
capable of establishing an understanding of how 
7 
that system transforms inputs into outputs.  
8 
Parties without access to confidential 
9 
information and therefore lacking such 
10 
information necessary for establishing an 
11 
understanding of the system, understand the 
12 
system merely as a "black box".  For the 
13 
purposes of my testimony, this type of "black 
14 
box"--one that arises from access restrictions 
15 
from existing information--can be best 
16 
understood as "locked box".   
17 
Q. 
Elaborate on the term "opaque box" and how you 
18 
define the term. 
19 
A. 
The difficulty and cost to analyze and define 
20 
processes increases as the complexity or scale 
21 
of a process increases.  For example, it may 
22 
require expending some resource for the Company 
23 
to produce a robust and precise explanation of 
24 


--- Page 271 ---
Cases 25-E-0072 and 25-G-0073 
 
ANDREW CARPENTER 
 
 
270  
 
Company's SCADA, the backbone of modern 
1 
infrastructure monitoring and management.  In 
2 
both internal and external communications 
3 
regarding the SCADA system, the Company must 
4 
necessarily determine what information, and with 
5 
what detail, to provide regarding the system.   
6 
The complexity of the system may also 
7 
necessitate, to some degree, the summarization 
8 
or abstraction of technical processes for 
9 
example, through charts and graphs visualizing 
10 
the output of function or simulation.  The 
11 
resource cost and summarization/abstraction of 
12 
technical processes present barriers to 
13 
understanding the "black box".  In these 
14 
circumstances, the “black box” is better 
15 
understood as an “opaque box” because with some 
16 
finite level of resource investment, what is 
17 
currently a black box can become completely 
18 
understood, meaning that the system processes 
19 
transforming inputs into outputs can be 
20 
completely understood conventionally.   
21 
A. 
Explain the term "true black box" and how you 
22 
define the term. 
23 
Q. 
A "true black box", for the purposes of this 
24 


--- Page 272 ---
Cases 25-E-0072 and 25-G-0073 
 
ANDREW CARPENTER 
 
 
271  
 
testimony, is a process or system of processes 
1 
with sufficient scale or complexity that the 
2 
processes cannot be fully and completely known 
3 
or cannot reasonably become fully or completely 
4 
known without significant resources or 
5 
significant time.  In the context of AI, "true 
6 
black boxes" are best represented by large 
7 
language models that can directly process and 
8 
interpret information.  These models can become 
9 
so complex that the exact processes cannot be 
10 
precisely understood; and so large that it 
11 
cannot be understood in a reasonable time.   
12 
Q. 
Absent sufficient information, should AI models 
13 
be presumed to be “true black boxes” or “opaque 
14 
boxes”? 
15 
A. 
Absent sufficient information, AI models should 
16 
be presumed to be “true black boxes”.  As the 
17 
process of explaining AI models with precision 
18 
is in nascency, presuming inexplicability is 
19 
more reasonable than presume explicability.  
20 
Conceptually, proving inexplicability is similar 
21 
to proving a negative; it is simple to prove 
22 
explicability insofar that the explanation is 
23 
the logical proof of a system’s explicability.  
24 


--- Page 273 ---
Cases 25-E-0072 and 25-G-0073 
 
ANDREW CARPENTER 
 
 
272  
 
For that reason, structuring the block analysis 
1 
such that AI systems are presumed to be “true 
2 
black boxes” and must be proven to be “opaque 
3 
boxes” is reasonable. 
4 
Q. 
How do these "boxes" fit in with the reporting 
5 
framework recommended you? 
6 
A. 
The reporting is intended to begin developing 
7 
the information necessary to classify the 
8 
Company's AI applications, systems, and 
9 
processes within the box structure with 
10 
increasing precision.  On-going, evolving 
11 
reporting is warranted given the nascency of AI, 
12 
AI testing methods, and AI oversight; coupled 
13 
with the rapid progression of AI, the ongoing 
14 
adoption of AI, and the interweaving of AI 
15 
within existing applications and services.  
16 
Fitting project elements within the box 
17 
structure provides a framework for evaluating 
18 
the merits and risks arising from an AI 
19 
application. 
20 
Q. 
Provide an example application of the block 
21 
analysis to an AI-related project. 
22 
A. 
The Company indicates in response to DPS-617 
23 
that for the project "Connected Vehicles - 
24 


--- Page 274 ---
Cases 25-E-0072 and 25-G-0073 
 
ANDREW CARPENTER 
 
 
273  
 
Enterprise Vehicle Telematics", the Company will 
1 
not own the AI model and will not have direct 
2 
access to the AI model code base.  As such, from 
3 
the Company's position— and by extension, the 
4 
Commission’s position— the AI Model is a "locked 
5 
box".  The character of the AI scope, which 
6 
provides "knowledge base", and states that the 
7 
AI can "offer real-time driver coaching", it can 
8 
be reasonably assumed that the AI Model is 
9 
sufficiently complex or of sufficient scale to 
10 
be considered an "opaque box".  Additional 
11 
technical information would be necessary to 
12 
determine whether the AI application described 
13 
represents a "true black box"; but absent 
14 
information proving that the AI Model is 
15 
understandable or explainable, it is more 
16 
reasonable to assume that it is not.  Taken 
17 
together, the project’s AI application is a 
18 
locked opaque box that should be assumed to be a 
19 
true black box.  
20 
A. 
What should the end-product of AI reporting 
21 
development provide? 
22 
Q. 
At minimum, AI reporting would identify all 
23 
"locked boxes" and "true black boxes"; and begin 
24 


--- Page 275 ---
Cases 25-E-0072 and 25-G-0073 
 
ANDREW CARPENTER 
 
 
274  
 
to quantify the opacity of "opaque boxes".  A 
1 
reasonable initial step in the process of 
2 
establishing adequate oversight over AI process 
3 
is the identification and tracking of elements 
4 
with restricted access -- "locked boxes"; 
5 
elements impossible to fully understand with 
6 
presently available conventional methods -- 
7 
"true black boxes"; and elements where analytic 
8 
methods are technically impractical or 
9 
constrained by resources -- "opaque boxes".  As 
10 
new methods for testing and evaluating AI models 
11 
arise, opaqueness will become quantifiable and 
12 
the number of "true black boxes" will decrease.   
13 
Q. 
What specific reporting do you recommend? 
14 
A. 
I recommend that the Commission direct ongoing 
15 
reporting in addition to the reporting 
16 
recommended for all IT projects.  I recommend 
17 
that the Commission direct Con Edison to develop 
18 
yearly reports that updates the Commission the 
19 
status of its AI projects.  We recommend that 
20 
this report be required to provide detailed 
21 
technical and financial information associated 
22 
with AI applications, including training costs, 
23 
descriptions of training data, current 
24 


--- Page 276 ---
Cases 25-E-0072 and 25-G-0073 
 
ANDREW CARPENTER 
 
 
275  
 
validation practices, an example use-case 
1 
demonstrating the nature of user input and model 
2 
output, the character and magnitude of model 
3 
drift, the frequency of model use by any 
4 
business decisions that rely upon or are 
5 
otherwise informed by the output of AI.  Given 
6 
that my recommended project budget retains 
7 
funding, in part, for the Data & AI Governance 
8 
Program, the Company is already positioned and 
9 
has funding to develop such information.   
10 
Q. 
Does this conclude your testimony at this time? 
11 
A. 
Yes. 
12
